@@115850460 <h> A 90% reduction in CO2 eq . emissions : a challenge that became a reality with Renault Embl ? me <p> Decarbonising mobility is a key objective in the fight against climate change . For an automotive company such as Renault Group , it involves addressing a number of challenges relating to industry , energy , technology , supply , speed , and so on . To achieve net zero carbon by 2040 in Europe and 2050 worldwide , in line with its targets , the company needs to revolutionise the way it designs and builds its vehicles . <p> This approach is demonstrated by Renault Embl ? me displayed by the brand on its stand at the Paris Motor Show from 14 to 20 October . This vision of a family vehicle is a laboratory showcasing the innovations developed by Renault , Ampere and their partners to meet these requirements . <p> The main challenge of this landmark project lies in the extent to which decarbonisation has been maximised across the entire life cycle of the vehicle , from cradle to grave . Following on from the Scenic Vision H2-Tech concept in 2022 , Renault Embl ? @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ gas ( CO2 eq. ) emissions by 90% , compared with an equivalent vehicle built today ; to produce just 5 tonnes of CO2 eq . from cradle to grave ( see methodology section at the end of the press release ) . <p> To achieve this , the project explored a multitude of intelligent , credible and viable combinations : recycled materials with a low carbon footprint , natural materials , production processes relying entirely on renewable energy , general implementation of re-used parts and circularity , etc . The same was true of the technical choices , including the powertrain . <h> Efficiency at the heart of Renault 's new design language <p> Renault Embl ? me symbolises a new holistic approach to vehicle design . Its exterior design blends sportiness , elegance and emotion through sensual curves and graphic , technical character lines , creating an ultra-desirable object . The design is also the result of meticulous optimisation of aerodynamics , designed for efficiency through a more sustainable approach without compromising on aesthetics . <p> On Renault Embl ? me , for example , the exterior rearview @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ arches , with windscreen wipers concealed under the bonnet and e-latch handles recessed into the bodywork . Two fins on the bonnet and two air vents on the bumper channel the airflow towards the windscreen and behind the wheels , respectively . <p> The wheels are full disc , in order to conduct the air flow along the body . The F1-inspired flat-bottom design is enhanced by an active diffuser , which tilts downwards and to the rear to balance the airflow and minimise aerodynamic drag . <p> The design was fine-tuned using digital twin technology and state-of-the-art digital simulation equipment provided by the BWT Alpine F1 Team , as part of a fruitful collaboration between Renault and Amp ? re . By pooling their expertise , they were able to optimise passive and active aerodynamics for efficient air penetration . Renault Embl ? me has a very good Cx of 0.25 . <h> The elegance of a family shooting brake making no compromises on efficiency <p> Renault Embl ? me 's strong curves and shooting brake style are showcased by the dichroic effect of the green bodywork , making @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ it is viewed . The vehicle is an elegant combination of an estate , with a wide wheelbase of 2.90 metres , and a coup ? with a moderate height of 1.52 metres and flowing roofline . <p> The vehicle is 4.80 metres long , making it ideal for comfortable family travel , with passengers and luggage . It also symbolises Renault 's reaffirmed ambition to continue innovating in the C segment and higher , in line with its value-based strategy . Exploring new paths in design inspired by the iconic Renault logo , the light signature is intended to be both high-tech and easily identifiable . <p> A vehicle 's weight , which is partially linked to its size , impacts its CO2 eq . emissions at several levels : when materials are extracted , during production and transport , during use ( impact on energy consumption ) and when the vehicle is recycled . As part of their eco-design approach , the designers and engineers hunted down every superfluous kilogram , with the aim of reaching a weight of 1,750 kg , including batteries . Renault Embl ? @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ vehicles of similar size . <h> A powertrain combining versatility and low carbon performance <p> Renault Embl ? me has a dual power energy electric powertrain powered in two ways : by a " conventional " rechargeable battery , sufficient for everyday use , and by a hydrogen fuel cell for longer journeys . Based on the AmpR Medium platform , the rear-wheel drive architecture hosts the various powertrain components ( electric motor , battery , fuel cell and hydrogen tank ) while maintaining a low centre of gravity and ideal weight distribution to promote performance and efficiency . <p> Embl ? me 's 160 kW wound-rotor electric motor uses no rare earths . It is powered by a small NMC battery ( 40 kWh ) , which is lighter , cheaper , less bulky and more environmentally friendly than that of a long-range family electric vehicle . It has a range of several hundred kilometres , more than enough for everyday journeys . The 30 kW PEMFC fuel cell runs on low-carbon hydrogen via a 2.8 kg tank , providing the power required for long journeys , with maximum @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ versatility of this type of vehicle , which is able to cover a distance of up to 1,000 km as rapidly as an ICE vehicle : without charging but with just two stops for hydrogen refuelling -- less than five minutes each time -- for a range of 350 km . On a typical journey between Paris and Marseille , 75% of the electricity consumed by the vehicle is produced by the fuel cell , with no emissions other than water . <h> An innovative working method <p> In view of the ambitious decarbonisation goals , the Embl ? me demo car required new working methods . More than 20 partners , all experts in their own fields , worked on the project with Renault and Ampere . Each one contributed their own technology or expertise to slash CO2 eq . emissions by 90% over the vehicle 's entire life cycle . <p> This collective horizontal approach encourages innovation without barriers between the Group , its brands , entities , partners and suppliers . For this project , this approach made it possible to harness exceptional technological diversity and complexity @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ me demo car shows once again that Renault Group is at the forefront of innovation , driving progress in mobility and the automotive industry in general , making its entire ecosystem part of the process . Ampere will unveil this car in full at the end of October . <p> A Kerryman from the Sliabh Luachra area , he has been working and living in Dublin for over 50 years . Raised on songs and stories and a Ford pick-up truck . Se ? n bought his first car , a second hand Ford Escort in 1973 and has been writing about new cars since 1998 . @@115981494 <h> Sainsbury 's Launches Peat-Free Mushrooms <p> UK retailer Sainsbury 's has launched peat-free mushrooms , which it claims is a first among supermarkets in the country . <p> The production process of the product is less carbon intensive as it protects nature by leaving peat on the ground , according to the retailer . <p> Currently , conventional mushroom production relies on the use of peat , a precious carbon-rich natural resource that takes thousands of years to grow in wetland ecosystems . <p> Mushrooms are one of the most popular fresh produce items with over 1.5 billion portions sold each year in the UK , the retailer added . <h> Peat-Free Mushrooms <p> The new mushroom SKU features ' Grown without Peat ' on the packaging and is available in more than 200 Sainsbury 's stores across the country . <p> ADVERTISEMENT <p> The first peat-free mushrooms will be White Closed Cup 300g and White Baby Button 200g variants . <p> Richard Crampton , director of fresh food at Sainsbury 's , stated , " At Sainsbury 's , we 're committed to playing our part in enabling and driving a resilient and sustainable food system , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Our new peat-free mushrooms will help us to get another step closer to achieving these goals in our supply chain . <p> " We 're proud to be the first supermarket in the UK to bring peat-free mushrooms to our customers , who want easy ways to make more responsible choices when buying food , without compromising on quality . This would n't have been possible without the work of our partner Monaghan who spent 10 years coming up with the alternative . " <h> Production Process <p> Mushrooms are typically grown in two layers of material -- first a layer of natural compost and then a layer of peat . <p> ADVERTISEMENT <p> Sainsbury 's peat-free mushrooms are grown with an innovative sustainable substrate made from recycled natural materials . <p> Developed in partnership with Sainsbury 's long-term mushroom supplier Monaghan , the new process will reduce the use of 20,465 tonnes of peat from mushroom production per year . <p> Peat a natural carbon storer and its extraction for growing products such as mushrooms is impacting wetland ecosystems and contributing to climate change . <h> ' Climate Impact @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ footprint in comparison to other foods , we want to take this further with our journey to net zero . Peat-free mushrooms are a huge step forward with that ambition . " <p> Sainsbury 's also claims to have launched Vitamin D enhanced white mushrooms after it launched vitamin-enhanced chestnut mushrooms in 2018 in the UK . <p> The retailer has pledged to achieve Net Zero across its own operations by 2035 , and supply chain by 2050.

@@116058794 <p> Cases of ' airport and luggage ' malaria are rising in Europe ( Image : Getty ) <p> Holidaymakers have been issued a stark warning as cases of a deadly disease carried by mosquitoes and transported on planes are on the rise in Europe . The instances of " suitcase and airport malaria " , also known as Odyssean malaria , have been detected in people returning from their holidays . <p> Out of 6,131 malaria cases reported in the EU/EEA in 2022 , a staggering 99 percent were travel-related . Odyssean malaria refers to cases that result from the bite of an infected mosquito transported by aircraft , luggage , or parcel from an area where malaria is prevalent . <p> Despite malaria being eradicated in western Europe around half a century ago , locally acquired infections are still identified . These include infections transmitted by a local mosquito after it has bitten an infected returning traveller , induced cases related to other means of transmission such as mother-to-child transmission , and Odyssean malaria . <p> The NHS has warned that if malaria is not diagnosed and treated promptly , it can be fatal . It only @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> According to the health body , malaria can be " hard to spot " , but symptoms include : <p> A high temperature , sweats and chills <p> Headaches and feeling confused <p> Feeling very tired and sleepy ( especially in children ) <p> Feeling and being sick , tummy pain and diarrhoea <p> Loss of appetite <p> Muscle pains <p> Yellow skin or whites of the eyes <p> A sore throat , cough and difficulty breathing . <p> These symptoms usually appear between seven and 18 days after you 've been bitten by an infected mosquito . " But sometimes you may not have symptoms for months after travel , and rarely years , " the NHS adds , reports the Mirror . <p> People in certain European countries may be at a higher risk of contracting malaria , according to a systematic review of studies from 1969 to January 2024 . The review revealed that out of 145 cases described from nine European countries , 105 were classified as airport malaria , 32 as luggage malaria , and eight as either type of malaria . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and Germany , with many of the affected individuals residing or working near an international airport . The review also showed that malaria cases have been on the rise in recent years , with one-third of cases reported over the last five years - even during the Covid pandemic . <h> Read More <h> Related Articles <h> Read More <h> Related Articles <p> A separate analysis of surveillance and case investigation data showed that there were 117 locally acquired malaria cases reported in France between 1995 to 2022 . Half of the people affected were born in a country in Africa , where malaria is common , and the other half were born in France . <p> This comes as some experts are warning that malaria cases will become more common in parts of the world previously unaffected due to climate change , as temperatures rise . To lower your risk of contracting malaria , the NHS recommends getting advice from a GP , nurse , pharmacist or travel clinic before you go to a country where malaria is common . <p> It only takes one bite from an infected @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ The health body advises : " It 's best to do this at least 4 to 6 weeks before you travel , but you can still get advice at the last minute if you need to , " adding , " You may be prescribed antimalarial tablets to reduce the risk of getting malaria and told how you can prevent mosquito bites . " <p> To further minimise risks , travellers are also advised that if they have recently visited a malaria-endemic country and are experiencing symptoms , seek immediate medical attention from your GP.

Indeed , the claim of the festival , which takes place in Salento , between Matino and Lecce , is " From Planet to Plate " . The focus is on the visual arts as an essential tool for rethinking the relationship between man and the environment . <p> According to Edda Fahrenhorst , the festival 's artistic director , " there are questions that may seem obvious for an essential , everyday activity like eating . What did you eat today ? Where did the ingredients come from ? Are they fresh or processed ? These are questions that invite us to reflect on our food , its provenance and sustainability " . <p> There are trends , for instance , that do not take into account that the production of some foods @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of water resources , or the use of pesticides , or that some crops , such as avocados , have been infiltrated by histories of organised crime . <p> But food is also culture . Through its history , we can trace the history of humanity as it has declined over time and place . It is also a sign of a techno-social system , which in turn has an impact on the climate and the economy . Yeast also presents education days on topics ranging from the circular economy to Transition 5.0 , sustainability in business , labour exploitation and supply chain screening ( environmental , social and governance factors ) . <p> To understand more , one can look at the images of the fifteen photographic projects on show here , in Salento , among the white houses and ancient underground oil mills of Matino and the Baroque of Lecce . @@116140331 <p> Agriculture was responsible for 37.8% of greenhouse gas ( GHG ) emissions in Ireland in 2023 , according to the EPA , thus the sector is key if we are to meet our emissions targets . IO Agri of Canningstown has a patented system , which takes this global GHG challenge and turns it into a valuable asset for farmers . <p> Co-founded in 2018 by husband and wife team , Sandra and Shane O'Reilly , the company 's initial focus was on providing agritech solutions to the pig and poultry sector . <p> " A downturn in the market came around 2020 , " recalls Sandra . " We saw planning applications being refused by local authorities because of greenhouse gas emissions . That made us pivot and our mission now is to reduce those gases coming from the animal housing units . <p> " A huge amount of gases comes from these units ; ammonia , CO2 , nitrogen , hydrogen and they are all being blown out into the atmosphere . Our system traps those gases and allows us to sell them on . Ammonia is the second most widely produced commodity chemical , globally @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ at their R&amp;D centre constructed in 2020 where the system has been independently analysed and certified . <p> " One of our main principles in IO Agri when we bring climate change solutions to the farming community is that we do not increase operational costs , but create an additional revenue stream for the farmer , so when we trap these gases we then sell them on , for example to fertiliser companies which they can use as an ingredient or to resell as clean ammonia and green hydrogen . " <p> IO Agri sees an opportunity to supply intensive agri farms , including beef and dairy systems on the continent , for example in Denmark , with their European distribution network . <p> With the Irish climate becoming increasingly wet , forcing cattle to be housed in sheds for much more of the year , the company anticipates much demand for its system in the future in the Irish context too . 
@@116197339 <p> Any food left out for the birds , can be protected using chili powder which prevents it being stolen and eaten by other animals who are often scavengers . <p> Gardeners have seen far fewer birds than normal and the RSPB says over the past four decades , one in six have been lost . <p> Gardeners have been advised to sprinkle chilli in their gardens ( Image : Reach PLC ) <p> This has been caused by a decline in insect numbers which have fallen by 80% n the last 20 years because of habitat loss and climate change . <p> The Express reports this is having knock-on effects further up the food chain because they have far less to eat and are at risk of starvation . <p> That 's why gardeners are being asked to do what they can for birds and leave bird food out . <p> But one huge problem with leaving bird food out is that other animals - like squirrels or foxes - will try to steal it . This is a common problem and there is not enough food to go around for those species either , <p> Fewer birds @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Getty Images ) <p> That 's where the chilli powder comes in . If you have a birdbath or bird table , or something which you leave bird food on for the birds and if you do n't have one , consider getting one , then you can leave a mix of peanuts , seeds , mealworms and slices of fruit like apple out for birds to eat . <p> Bruised apples and pears are really attractive to birds , and household scraps like pastry , cooked rice and breadcrumbs can be used too - although you should avoid using too much of these , say the RSPB . <p> But lace all of it with chilli powder , and the birds will have the food all to themselves . That 's because birds are n't at all affected by chilli powder , whereas squirrels and foxes hate it , which allows you to leave all the food for the birds . <p> The RSPB says : " When buying bird food , try to get a good mix of peanuts , seeds and live food like mealworms and waxworms @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ pears , will be popular with thrushes and Blackbirds . Household scraps like pastry , cooked rice and breadcrumbs should only be offered in small amounts occasionally . " <p> " Strong chilli powder or pepper sauce ( like Tabasco ) can be shaken onto bird food . Birds are n't bothered by the chilli , but most squirrels ca n't put up with the burning sensation and will leave the food alone . I think we are used to having to fight for ourselves , fight for what we can get , and just standing up for ourselves . ' <p> In the letter @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to reconsider the partnership in light of the governing body 's commitment to combating climate change , given that Aramco is the world 's largest state-owned oil company and has a history of lobbying to delay climate action . <p> In it , the players write of the floods , extreme heat and fires that are ' burning football 's future ' , before saying that ' as we all pay the consequences Saudi Arabia rakes in its profits , with FIFA as its cheerleader . ' <p> The letter also queries how LGBTQ+ players , ' many of whom are heroes of our sport ' , can be expected to promote Aramco , given that Saudi Arabia criminalises LGBTQ+ relationships . <p> ' There are so many aspects to this . There is also the state ownership of football clubs and the number of players who have moved to Saudi for their league , ' Caldwell says . ' As footballers , and playing the world 's biggest sport , we are in an unique position to highlight this . ' <p> Inter Milan 's Sofie Junge Pedersen , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ one of the drivers of this letter and she feels that this sponsorship deal is like a step backwards after all the strides forward that the women 's game has made in recent years . <p> ' As we write in the letter , FIFA 's choice to have Aramco as a sponsor helps the Saudi regime distract from their harmful treatment of women and the planet , ' Pedersen said . <p> ' As signatories , we are saying that we will not be part of distracting from these violations . Values are not just words to write on a page -- you need to live them and stand by them . @@116658423 <h> Paula Hynes : A visit from a scholar <p> We are no strangers to having visitors on the farm , and this week , we had a UK visitor . Hattie McFadzean is a senior sustainability consultant with Promar International which is a leading provider of consultancy for farmers , food producers and retailers . Companies like that are crucial when it comes to sustainability as they understand and work with the entire supply chain . <p> Hattie worked in southeast Asia for a number of years working closely with the agricultural sector and now living just outside London , she has a passion for working closely with stakeholders in the Ag sector to improve transparency of information regarding sector emissions and opportunities for green growth . <p> She certainly understands the supply chain because after a brief tour of the farm , she was in the milking parlour with us and is a dab hand at putting on clusters . <p> People like Hattie are a huge asset to the agri industry . As we rise to the challenge of reducing emissions , it is crucial to have people who can come on farm , work alongside @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and then take that knowledge up through the supply chain as when we all play our part in sustainability , the results are cumulative . <p> Ag often gets a bad name when it comes to climate change . The farmer gets a bit of a bashing in the media at times , but the reality is there is no point in the farmer doing all the work to reduce emissions whilst at the other end of the supply chain , there is a stockpile of food waste which believe it or not also emits methane . <p> The farmer is working with climate change every day , battling changing weather and seasonal changes and trying to adapt how they farm to deal with those changes whilst also adapting new ways of farming to reduce emissions . <p> It is not just a one-solution fix . Changes are being made to breeding practices , genetics , feed additives , slurry additives , slurry management , fertilisers , crop production , energy use and production ... the list goes on . <p> Likewise , the food processor is looking at new @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the retail sector , transport from farm to processors and onwards to supermarkets . <p> Those retail outlets and restaurants are also adapting to become greener so that the food in the consumer 's shopping basket or dinner plate is more climate-friendly than anything they have eaten before . <p> Our chat with Hatttie meandered from the milking parlour , to the dinner table and continued over a glass of wine . She is a Nuffield Scholar , and her topic is ' Enhancing the resilience of the dairy sector , how do we practically adapt to increasingly volatile climate conditions ' . <p> It truly is inspirational to see her visiting so many farms . Research can deliver so much , but it is how easy the farmer can implement that research on-farm daily that counts and , at the same time , taking into account the changing environment that the farmer must also work in . Amongst all that , the greatest sustainability question of all is can the farmer make a sustainable living and afford to invest in reducing emissions . <p> As part of her scholarship @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ spent the last two weeks in Ireland and will venture to India , which is home to the largest amount of dairy cows in the world . <p> A trip to an African country is also a possibility and , a must , in my opinion . While we constantly talk about reducing Ag emissions , we often forget how important food security is within that conversation . Those in third world countries might not have access to the same technology , but food , in many cases , is mere survival but they are also the people who are even more impacted by climate change and need to adapt how they produce food while also doing it in a sustainable manner . <p> After all the great chat about global farming and Irish dairy , it was an early start for us all the following morning as Hattie was heading east to visit an inspirational organic farm manager , Kevin O'Hanlon . Weather has been mild up to now so we will carry a nice cover of grass into the winter , which will be welcome next spring for early calving cows . <p> Midterm break is upon us now and the weather looks settled after the bank holiday weekend with high pressure building so our maize silage will be harvested early in the week , crops have been slow to ripen this autumn , I suppose the lack of sunshine throughout the summer and mild weather lately has delayed ripening . <p> It will be a first @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is another way of how we are adapting our farm and changing calving patterns . @@116659814 <h> Why is the civil service so far behind in reporting greening government progress ? <p> Do as we do , not just as we say . The mantra that government must lead by example when it comes to mitigating climate change -- by continually improving its own sustainability record -- became a driver of practical measures under the coalition government back in 2010 . <p> A series of targets were set out , known as the Greening Government Commitments ( GGCs ) , requiring 22 government departments and their arm 's length bodies to reduce environmental impacts from water consumption , landfill waste and business flights . The measures also required an increase in carbon-cutting measures across the civil estate , from offices to warehouses and prisons . <p> With more than 500,000 staff across the UK , the civil service produces almost 2m tonnes of carbon dioxide ( CO2 ) a year : a carbon footprint similar to that of Sheffield . In the wider public sector , NHS emissions alone make up 4% of England 's total carbon footprint . Trimming the carbon fat from these operations is not just politically symbolic ; it is also @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , which includes overall and departmental targets . Current progress , though , is uncertain . Reporting -- due for each financial year -- is now three years in arrears . The most recent GGC data , for 2020-21 , was published in spring 2023 . Despite a demand that the 2021-22 data -- let alone the 2022-23 and 2023-24 figures -- be published early this year , there is no sign of them . Defra is unable to give even the vaguest commitment as to when we might learn how the civil service is doing . <p> Last year , the government was excoriated for its tardiness by the then-chair of the Environmental Audit Committee , Philip Dunne , who said his committee had lost patience over the issue , that Covid-19 was no longer an excuse for feet-dragging and that he expected to see the 2021-22 figures published " well before April 2024 " . <p> A spokesperson for Defra responded : " April 2024 was not a date we committed to . The publication of 2021-22 data was delayed by the pre-election period and a new government @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ be given for when any data would be published , and whether the 2024 general election was an acceptable reason for the delay ( since the election was not called until two months after the April deadline had passed ) , the spokesperson said only : " The data will be published in due course . " <p> Defra has issued quarterly updates on its own progress towards sustainability commitments . Its latest update , published in April 2024 ( covering October -- December 2021 ) was limited in scope but showed it had moved forward with plans for installation of wind-turbines , solar water heaters and ground source heat pumps into several of its sites , and was " committing to low carbon technology replacements when required " . <p> The actual numbers in the report raised questions over how meaningful these steps had been , however . Defra fell short of its direct emissions reduction target of 15% ( against a 2017-18 baseline ) , cutting it only by 13% . Efforts to cut overall emissions ( including transport and grid emissions ) fell even shorter , amounting @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ reduction in emissions . <p> The National Audit Office continues to shine a light on various departments and how they implement GGCs . In its June 2023 report , Department for Education : sustainability overview , the NAO identified education as the public sector 's largest emitter of carbon from buildings , amounting to 37% of public sector emissions . This comprises 13% from state primary schools , 11% from state secondary schools and 13% from universities . The NAO found that DfE had set up internal structures and processes to enable it " to manage , monitor and deliver a range of initiatives " but that the department was " still developing governance and accountability measures , and raising awareness of its aims both within DfE and the sector more generally " . <p> " Covid was an issue but there is no excuse for not publishing every year " Phillip Dunne , former Environmental Audit Committee chair <p> More detail emerged on GGCs in the State of the Estate report for 2022-23 , which was published via the Cabinet Office in December 2023 . By the end of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ by 38% against the 2017-18 baseline -- an improvement on the 35% reduction achieved in 2021-22 . Direct emissions from government buildings were reduced by 12% against the 2017-18 baseline , an improvement on the 9% reduction achieved in 2021-22 , despite more departmental staff returning to their place of work following the Covid-19 pandemic . Reductions in overall energy consumption saved the government an estimated ? 163m when compared with the 2017-18 baseline . <p> Overall , government reduced total waste by 16% from the 2017-18 baseline , the report found , exceeding the 15% target . On an individual basis , the 15% target was met or exceeded by 15 out of the 20 departments that reported . In total , 5% of departmental waste was sent to landfill which met the 5% maximum target . Yet only 51% of government waste was recycled , falling short of the 70% target . <p> The government 's water consumption reduced by 5% in 2022-23 compared to the baseline , missing the 8% reduction target that was previously met in 2021-22 ( where water consumption reduced by 9% ) . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ met or exceeded the target to reduce water consumption by 8% compared to the baseline . Reductions in water consumption saved the government an estimated ? 3m . <p> Departments are now working towards new goals outlined in the 2021-2025 GGC Framework ( published in December 2022 ) , which recalibrated the baseline to 2017-18 and set out the overarching aim of " mitigating climate change and working towards net zero by 2050 " . Emissions reduction targets for 2024-25 in relation to the 2017-18 baseline include DfT ( 62% ) , DHSC ( 44% ) , HMT ( 69% ) , HMRC ( 60% ) and MoD ( 30% ) . <p> Dunne assumes civil servants will already have briefed ministers about the GGC reporting requirements . Speaking to CSW , he said : " Covid was an issue , and some departments have large carbon footprints because they are based in historic buildings -- but there is no excuse for not publishing GGCs every year , " he says . " The GGCs matter . The new administration has set very high expectations for how they are going @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ other elements of society to change behaviour , but when they do , it 's always more credible if they apply that to their own aspects of environmental policy . " <p> During his time chairing the EAC , Dunne recalls how reviews of GGC progress at the MoD , DfE and DHSC , undertaken with the NAO , found " varying degrees of compliance and capacity to comply " but that the audits " sharpened pencils " , and departments " found it useful to have an external force encouraging them to comply . The pace at which compliance can happen is often dictated by funding priorities , so all eyes will be on the budget to see the extent to which the in-house greening agenda is supported " . 
@@116688898 <h> World Aviation Festival returns to Amsterdam this October bringing the biggest names in aviation to town <p> The aviation industry faced some of the toughest conditions imaginable after the spread of COVID-19 , with more than 20 air carriers forced to suspend services entirely . <p> Yet while progress picked up again slowly at first , the latest reports from 2023 suggest that the effects of the pandemic are now firmly behind us . The IATA estimated that last year the airline industry generated $23bn net profit , with an extremely strong summer also expected this year . <p> Although these are positive indicators , the industry still has challenges ahead that need to be addressed collectively . Current geopolitical tensions have had an impact on flight paths , with the closure of Russian airspace increasing costs for many carriers . @@116769354 <h> An expert 's guide to treasure hunting for second hand home furniture <h> Second hand furniture can add a unique edge to your home , as well as being better for the environment , and often easier on your wallet . Interior designer Deirdre O'Connell shares her tips for sourcing a great find in Ireland . <p> Scouring for second-hand furniture is n't for everyone , but its advantages have never been so great . There is the obvious , generally large , financial saving , all the while doing your major bit for climate change , but with global material shortages and long , changeable , shipping delays , wait time for decent furniture can now regularly be 25 , 30 , even 40 weeks . <p> For this variety of reasons , more and more of us are turning to local vintage , antique and " pre-loved " finds to furnish our homes , and with a little thought , it can achieve a gorgeous look . <p> As a designer , I avoid anything too perfect , or anything too dated and fussy . I like contemporary schemes , of bright rooms with charming furniture @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ old and the new . <p> To find treasures of old , I 've made a list of the places you can source a range of furniture , collectibles , homewares , and bric-a-brac . <h> Online <p> Ireland has wonderful , second-hand marketplaces like Done Deal , Adverts.ie , and Gumtree , which are free to sell on and easy to navigate . In the search bars , use brand names or shops , like " Neptune " , " Bo Concept " , " Casey 's Furniture " or " Ventura " to discover any items from valued furniture names . <p> A more specific furniture marketplace is the charming Gaff Shop Interiors . They ask sellers to photograph their items really well , for a tidy shop-front look to its webpage . I feel a number of vintage and antique trade sellers use this page too so there are often new items for sale . <p> I check these sites regularly and find they often have ex display goodies or furniture from show homes . You 'll find seriously good bargains . When you see a good @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ see if they have other interesting items . If you find an item that 's almost perfect , remember small tweaks are easy to make . If the leg colour of an armchair is wrong , they can be sprayed or stained to match your other room finishes . A new lampshade refreshes an old lamp . Some contemporary eucalyptus twigs transform a plain coloured , tall vase . <p> Outside of Ireland , eBay , Etsy , and 1st Dibs , all list vintage home delights on their vast marketplaces , although postage and delivery fees are often high . <p> The latter , 1st Dibs , is world-class in its vintage designer listings and is regularly used by interior designers for antique and modern furniture and you 'll see its name regularly in the sourcebooks of design magazines . Note , even vintage designer furniture does n't come cheap ; I like to browse , and I start my search from " Price Low To High " , you 'll get the gist ! <h> Social Media <p> Facebook Marketplace features homeware items and it is a quick @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ location . <p> Instagram is a design-lovers haven and there are a number of nice Irish , second-hand home furnishing pages . Refunk sells nicely upcycled pieces , and for bargain hunters , ShopDtale is a collection of second-hand home objects and wall d ? cor . <h> The Charity Shops <p> Who does n't love a good rummage ? ! Irish charity shops are second to none for their inexpensive , pre-used furniture and homeware . Thankfully the rooting is n't as intense as the clothes rummaging , as most charity shops have a corner home section in store , with bric-a-brac and smaller accessories , and often with a 50% sale on . <p> The dedicated home furnishing charity shops are the jewel in the crown . Oxfam Home on Francis Street in Dublin is a huge shop with space for sofas , tables , wardrobes and sideboards . <p> NCBI have a number of dedicated home and furniture warehouses , most notably on Francis Street ; a huge unit in Cherry Orchard and its largest home store on Mungret Street in Limerick . <h> The Antique Shops @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Saturday stroll in Dublin 8 allows you to visit the charity stores , and the gorgeous Irish antique shops , all in one afternoon . <p> These galleries of renowned furniture are distinctly more specialist and historically important ( with price tags to match ! ) but vintage nonetheless and a source of great inspiration if you 're passing through . <p> Do n't miss the wonderful Anonymous at the end of Francis Street for their quirky , colourful collection of mid century and art deco vibe furniture , cool posters and wall art . <p> Outside the Pale , the treasure trove that is The Store Yard in Portlaoise ( and an outlet in Youghal , Cork too ) is an interior designer 's dream and worth a trip to the midlands specifically ! Its huge warehouse , full of rooms of salvaged , vintage , period , antique and retro furniture , along with stores of fabrics , curios and garden stuff , is unbeatable . <p> While you 're in Laois , do n't miss Acquired 's awesome , mid-century showroom in Stradbally , in the Millers @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ heart of the village . <h> The Markets <p> While we all miss the famous Brocante Market at Dublin Liberties and the now , closed Harold 's Cross Bazaar , if you 've a car , and a very understanding buddy , drag them around West Dublin for a few hours . A word of warning , these flea markets are not furniture-specific but you 'd never know what you might spot in the general rough and tumble . <p> A Saturday jaunt to The Glen Abbey Market in Tallaght is a life experience . With a few furniture and homeware ' stalls ' , I picked up some nice , intact coffee table design books here . <p> Delia 's Gallery off the Long Mile Road is an absolute gem of second hand stuff . They clear-out old houses and business premises , so amongst all the ' stock ' of old beds , lockers , tables and office chairs , you might find a few nice accessories or lamps . This one ai n't for the faint hearted ! <p> Vintage Ireland are also running their antiques pop-up @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> On the northside of Cork city , the Victorian Quarter has the pretty Mother Jones Flea Market which has a number of dedicated homeware and accessories stalls , and just across the road , do n't miss Salvagem Antique Furniture store which carries some seriously beautiful fine antiques and collectables and a gorgeous old store . <h> The Salvage Yards <p> For bigger ticket furniture items and home building and garden ware , check out the salvage yards . The mothership , Macs Warehouse in Dublin 8 never fails to impress for church pews , stained glass windows and commercial steel kitchens ( ! ) and they carry some newer items like mirrors and funky garden d ? cor . <p> You also pass BG Salvage in Naas , a mass of fine salvaged particulars . <p> All the salvage shops list online , but a day trip ( with an empty car boot ) is more rewarding . <h> The Circle <p> There is a great sub-culture to the second-hand furniture world . In my experience , things tend to be memorable ; you 'll have a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , or you 'll learn the hard way about measuring up ! <p> While there are obviously no refunds with second hand furniture , it 's worth remembering that whatever you have , you can resell ! Photograph it and list it again on Done Deal , Gaff or Adverts . I 've become as much a seller as I am a buyer . <p> I am always an advocate of guiding clients to resell old furniture , those lucky enough to have money for full refurbishments tend to need to get rid of furniture in advance . Unfortunately redecorating often means out with the old , in with the new , so the second-hand world often gets amazing items . <p> And all to keep that circle economy turning ! <p> Deirdre O'Connell is a Dublin based freelance interior designer and design writer . www.dtale.design

@@116851181 <p> Bangladesh , traditionally known for its robust garment factory and fast fashion , has experienced a significant shift in recent years . The country , once primarily focused on manufacturing apparel for global brands , is now emerging as a major player in the world of fashion design . Local designers are blending Bangladesh 's rich cultural heritage with modern trends , creating a new wave of innovation and initiative that is capturing the attention of the global fashion industry . With a strong focus on sustainability , traditional craftsmanship , and cutting-edge creativity , Bangladeshi designers are taking the world by storm . <p> Join https : //moreeeglory.com/ as we explore the rise of Bangladesh 's fashion revolution and supply chain , highlighting key designers , trends , and the factors contributing to the country 's growing influence on the global fashion stage . <p> 1 . From Garment Manufacturing to Fashion Design : The Key Findings <p> The Backbone of the Global Garment Industry <p> For decades , Bangladesh has been one of the leading garment sector exporters in the world , with the Ready-Made Garment ( RMG ) sector serving as a vital part of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , Zara , and Gap have relied on Bangladesh for the production of affordable , high-quality clothing . However , the focus on manufacturing sector often overshadowed the country 's potential as a hub for the labour of the original design . The production process of the garment factories in Bangladesh aims to develop the social and environmental working environment of the labour force and innovate the fashion brand organization of Bangladesh . <p> The Rise of Local Fashion Designers <p> In recent years , Bangladesh has shifted its focus from being a low-cost manufacturer to becoming a center of fashion creativity . A new generation of designers is harnessing the country 's rich cultural heritage and traditional craftsmanship to create fashion that resonates on both local and global stages . Designers are incorporating traditional elements like Nakshi Kantha ( embroidered quilts ) and Jamdani ( handwoven muslin ) into contemporary designs , redefining Bangladeshi fashion . <p> As the world looks for more sustainable and ethically sourced fashion , Bangladeshi designers are stepping into the spotlight , offering a fresh perspective that challenges the fast-fashion paradigm . <p> 2 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Jamdani : The Queen of Muslin <p> One of Bangladesh 's most prized traditional textiles is Jamdani , a handwoven fabric that dates back to the Mughal era . Known for its intricate patterns and lightweight texture , Jamdani is often referred to as the " queen of muslin . " Traditionally used for saris , this fabric has gained international recognition for its craftsmanship and beauty . <p> Designers like Bibi Russell , one of Bangladesh 's most influential fashion pioneers , have brought Jamdani into the global fashion spotlight . Bibi Russell , a former international model turned designer , has been instrumental in promoting Bangladeshi artisans and preserving traditional crafts . Her collections showcase Jamdani in modern , innovative ways , blending heritage with contemporary fashion . <p> Nakshi Kantha : Embroidery with a Fuel Story <p> Another traditional craft that has become a symbol of Bangladesh 's fashion renaissance is Nakshi Kantha , a type of embroidery known for its storytelling motifs . Originating in rural Bengal , Nakshi Kantha involves stitching old saris and cloths together with intricate , colorful patterns that often depict folk @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ designers have embraced Nakshi Kantha , using it as a form of artistic expression in modern clothing , accessories , and home decor . The resurgence of this craft not only highlights Bangladesh 's cultural heritage but also creates opportunities for rural artisans to participate in the global fashion economy . <p> Dhakai Muslin : A Lost Art Reimagined <p> Dhakai Muslin , once considered one of the finest fabrics in the world , was a symbol of luxury during the Mughal era . However , due to colonial exploitation and industrialization , this artisanal craft nearly disappeared . In recent years , efforts have been made to revive the lost art of Dhakai Muslin , with designers and organizations working to bring this exquisite fabric back into the global fashion conversation . <p> By reintroducing Dhakai Muslin into contemporary fashion , Bangladeshi designers are paying homage to the country 's textile history while creating new possibilities for sustainable luxury fashion . <p> As the global fashion industry becomes more conscious of its environmental and social impact , Bangladesh 's fashion designers are positioning themselves as leaders in the sustainable @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ textiles and artisanal craftsmanship aligns perfectly with the global demand for slow fashion , where quality , sustainability , and ethical production take center stage . <p> Designers like Maheen Khan , the founder of the Bangladesh Fashion Council , have been at the forefront of promoting sustainable fashion in the country . Her designs often incorporate eco-friendly materials and techniques , highlighting the importance of ethical fashion in the face of climate change and overconsumption . <p> Supporting Local Artisans and Craftspeople <p> One of the key components of Bangladesh 's fashion revolution is the commitment to supporting local artisans and craftspeople . Many designers are working directly with rural communities to preserve traditional crafts and create sustainable livelihoods for artisans . This focus on ethical production not only ensures fair wages and working conditions but also helps to protect Bangladesh 's cultural heritage . <p> For example , Anokhi , a brand known for its focus on traditional textiles , collaborates with local weavers and embroiders to create collections that are both contemporary and rooted in Bangladeshi tradition . This approach to fashion celebrates the skills of artisans @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ modern world . <p> 4 . Bangladeshi Designers Making Waves on the Global Stage <p> Bibi Russell : The Pioneer of Bangladeshi Fashion <p> Bibi Russell is often credited with spearheading Bangladesh 's entry into the global fashion scene . After a successful international modeling career , Russell returned to Bangladesh to launch her label , Bibi Productions , which focuses on promoting local artisans and preserving traditional crafts . Her collections along with her team of garment workers , which feature handwoven textiles like Jamdani and Nakshi Kantha , have been showcased on runways in Paris , London , and Milan . <p> Russell 's work has been recognized not only for its beauty but also for its social impact . She has been awarded numerous accolades for her contributions to fashion and development , including UNESCO 's Artist for Peace title . <p> Sarah Karim : A New Voice in Luxury Fashion <p> Sarah Karim , one of Bangladesh 's rising fashion stars , is known for her luxurious bridal and couture designs . Her label , Sarah Karim Couture , blends traditional Bangladeshi elements with modern aesthetics @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ both local and international markets . Karim 's attention to detail and commitment to craftsmanship have earned her a place among the top designers in South Asia . <p> Her collections often feature embellishments , intricate embroidery , and the use of rich fabrics like silk and velvet . By combining traditional techniques with contemporary designs , Karim is redefining luxury fashion in Bangladesh . <p> Emdad Haque : The Face of Avant-Garde Fashion <p> Emdad Haque is known for his avant-garde approach to fashion , pushing boundaries with his experimental designs and bold aesthetics . His collections often incorporate unconventional materials , blending streetwear with haute couture elements . Haque 's unique vision has earned him recognition on international runways , where his innovative approach to fashion has drawn attention from industry insiders . <p> Haque 's work demonstrates the versatility of Bangladeshi fashion , showing that the country 's designers are not limited to traditional garments but can also lead in cutting-edge , avant-garde fashion . <p> 5 . Bangladesh 's Fashion Weeks : Showcasing Talent to the World <p> Dhaka Fashion Week <p> Dhaka Fashion Week has @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ talent to the world . Held annually in the capital city , this event brings together local designers , international buyers , and media to highlight the country 's burgeoning fashion scene . From contemporary streetwear to traditional saris , Dhaka Fashion Week reflects the diversity and creativity of Bangladesh 's fashion industry . <p> Bangladesh Fashionology Summit <p> The Bangladesh Fashionology Summit is an innovative event that focuses on the intersection of fashion and technology . This summit aims to position Bangladesh as a hub for technological innovation in fashion , exploring topics such as smart textiles , 3D printing , and sustainable production methods . It serves as a platform for local designers and tech entrepreneurs to collaborate , pushing the boundaries of what is possible in the world of fashion . <p> 6 . The Role of Fashion in Empowering Women in Bangladesh <p> Women Leading the Fashion Industry <p> One of the most exciting aspects of Bangladesh 's fashion revolution is the role it has played in empowering women . Many of the country 's leading designers , entrepreneurs , and artisans are women , who @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and promote gender equality . Designers like Maheen Khan and Bibi Russell have not only achieved international success but have also become role models for young women in Bangladesh . <p> Creating Opportunities for Rural Women <p> The fashion industry in Bangladesh has also created opportunities for rural women to become economically independent . Many designers work directly with female artisans , providing them with training , fair wages , and access to global markets . This collaboration between designers and rural artisans has led to the development of sustainable communities , where women can contribute to the economy while preserving traditional crafts . <p> 7 . Challenges and Opportunities in the Global Market <p> Overcoming Stereotypes <p> Despite the growing recognition of Bangladeshi designers , the country still faces challenges in overcoming its image as merely a low-cost garment manufacturing hub . With continued investment in innovation and a focus on ethical production , Bangladesh 's fashion revolution is set to grow even stronger in the coming years . <p> Conclusion <p> Bangladesh 's fashion revolution is more than just a trend -- it is a movement that combines the country 's rich cultural heritage with modern innovation . @@116864508 <h> Investing in Election Years <h> With the US election fast approaching , many investors are growing increasingly wary of potential turbulence in the market . <p> The implications of a Trump or Harris presidency vary greatly , with contrasting policies on everything from trade tariffs to climate change . However , for markets , the impact is often less than dramatic . By Conor Murtagh , Investment Associate , Davy . <p> In Table 1 below , we look back on previous elections beginning with Roosevelt 's first term in 1932 . We can observe that , on average , the S&amp;P 500 's returns in the 12-month period before and after elections do not materially differ . Even when compared to non-election years , we do n't typically see abnormal returns in US markets -- for non-election years the mean return is 9.28% , while in years with an election we see a mean return of 8.85% . <p> During the 2016 race , investors clamoured over a number of so-called " Trump Trades " in an attempt to profit from the result of the election . This involved investing in industries which were set to benefit @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , and defense companies . Although some of the trades briefly succeeded in the days following the election , most failed to work at all . <p> Positioning investments around elections is difficult to get right and often leaves investors worse off than they previously were . From the chart below , it 's clear that , in the long run the winning strategy for investors has been staying invested , regardless of who is in the White House . The relentless growth of US equities has been agnostic to politics and will likely continue to be , no matter the result on 5 November . While election years can spur uncertainty in demand for steel , the impact on the construction industry will depend on policy and economic decisions . Clarity following an election can reinvigorate positive sentiment .

@@116889650 <h> Trinity scientists find surprising genetic diversity in early cows <p> Julius Ceaser once called the aurochs ' extraordinary ' . Now ancient DNA is unlocking secrets of this mysterious beast . <p> A genetic analysis of aurochs -- the predecessors to modern cattle -- led by Prof Dan Bradley at Trinity College Dublin 's ( TCD ) School of Genetics and Microbiology has revealed that the extinct cattle species was more genetically diverse than previously thought . <p> Aurochs , famous from early-human cave paintings , went extinct approximately 400 years ago , leaving much of their evolutionary history a mystery . <p> However , the TCD study , in collaboration with international partners , analysed 38 genomes harvested from aurochs bones dating across 50 millennia and spread from Siberia to Britain . The researchers found that the European aurochs , who were previously considered as just one type , actually consisted of three distinct populations -- Western European , Italian and Balkan . <p> " Through the sequencing of ancient DNA , we have gained detailed insight into the diversity that once thrived in the wild as well as enhanced our understanding of domestic cattle , " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the project study just published in Nature Communications . <p> Although fossils of aurochs found in Europe date back 650,000 years ago , around the time when early human species appeared on the continent , aurochs from the east and west extremes of Eurasia share a much more recent common ancestry -- indicating a migration from a South Asian homeland around 100,000 years ago , the study revealed . <h> Effect of climate change and human activities <p> The last ice-age that started around 100,000 years ago seemed to caused the European and North Asian aurochs populations to diverge . Genomic estimations showed that the aurochs population dropped during the glacial period , with the European herds being hit particularly hard . <p> The two populations did not mix until the world warmed up again at the ice-age 's ending , about 11,000 years ago . <p> Moreover , human domestication after the ice age caused the strongest drop in genetic diversity among the different aurochs herds . Only a handful of maternal lineages ( as seen via mitochondrial DNA which is handed down via mothers to their offspring ) @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , the researchers note . <p> " However , the narrow genetic base of the first cattle was augmented as they first travelled with their herders west , east and south , " Bradley said . <p> " It is clear that there was early and pervasive mating with wild aurochs bulls , leaving a legacy of the four separate preglacial aurochs ancestries that persists among the domestic cattle of today . " <p> Julius Caesar once described aurochs as " little below the elephant in size " . He was amazed at the animal 's strength and speed , calling it " extraordinary " . <p> " They spare neither man nor wild beast which they have espied , " he said . <p> " Although Caesar exaggerated when he said it was like an elephant , " Bradley said , " the wild ox must have been a highly dangerous beast and this hints that its first capture and taming must have happened with only a very few animals . " <p> Ancient DNA is unlocking many secrets of the past for researchers . Earlier this year , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ into an 8,000-year-old sheep bone that uncovered secrets about a harmful bacteria that may have evolved along with the development of farming and typically affects sheep , goats and humans . <p> Do n't miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed . My parents plied us with books and I read like there was no tomorrow . My first foray into writing was for school and college magazines . Where did the idea come from ? <p> The Inheritance is set in the Beara Peninsula in West Cork . The kernel of the idea came from an overheard conversation at a kitchen table and I stewed on it for 20 years . Set in 1986 , the story centres around a young man of London Irish stock who inherits a cottage in Glengarriff , coming to Ireland at a time when most young men are leaving the country . A thread from the 1600s drawing on the historic Long March of O'Sullivan Beare and runs through the book . The two stories , though separated by 400 years , are tied together as my characters seek their identities and find peace as they come to terms with loss and the power of forgiveness . <h> What do you hope this book instils in the reader ? <p> Hope and the ultimate joy that springs from it @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ this hidden history of O'Sullivan Beare 's time in the forests of Glengarriff -- enough to want to walk and explore the longest way marked trail in Ireland , the stunning Beara-Breifne Way . <h> What did you learn when writing this book ? <p> It was a refresher course in the attitudes of the 80s ! Ireland was altogether a different country . Research taught me a lot of history and geography too ! The Tudor conquest of Ireland was extremely cruel and our landscape played a huge role in shaping the outcome of the struggle . Reading about the flora and fauna of Beara opened up a whole new window into the effects of climate change on the coast of Ireland and how invasive species like rhododendrons are the most terrible of beauties . <h> Tell us about your writing process ? <p> I 'm always full of good intentions to write a little every day , but of course I do n't -- life gets in the way . So my writing can be a bit chaotic but my best work is done in Beara itself . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and exercise in one go . I never plot my novels and trust entirely in the voices of my characters to take me wherever they want . So writing by headlights is my method -- I can see two pages ahead but not any further ! <h> Where do you draw inspiration from ? <p> Observing people and places -- that 's my inspiration . <h> What are your top three favourite books of all time , and why ? <p> A Fine Balance by Rohington Mistry . Set in Mumbai , India a very compassionate but visceral telling of hope in the depths of despair . <p> The Poisonwood Bible by Barbara Kingsolver . I loved how she laid bare the utter arrogance of religious colonialism through the prism of very believable fictional characters . <p> The Prophet by Khalil Gibran . I dip into my 40 year old copy often and always find some wisdom to dwell on . 
@@116890947 <h> How to disappear <p> Have you ever wondered how difficult it would be to erase all trace of your existence ? <p> I admit that 's not something any of us normally grapple with when we have an idle moment , not when there are other things like " did I leave the iron on ? " or " I need to remember to get some milk next time I 'm out " or " how will we cope with our star player out injured ? " <p> Existential issues do n't often get a look in , which probably explains why climate change , for example , is n't top of the global agenda and a priority for all world leaders . <p> advertisement <p> Anyway to return to the question , how hard is it to erase all trace of your existence ? Is it possible to make it as if you never happened ? <p> I was prompted to ask this question because of a press release which claimed 36% of people surveyed in the UK wanted to delete themselves from the Internet and limit their digital footprint . Perhaps most surprisingly , Gen Z @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ them wanting to be deleted from it . <p> Now , I 'll admit that 's not quite as drastic as erasing yourself entirely from existence but in an increasingly digital age , it 's going quite far along the way . <p> The strange thing is that it 's very hard to remove all traces of ourselves because we have so many digital interactions every day and because so many records are kept about us . It 's not like the days when a fire in an office could delete someone 's birth record , for example . Paper records appear more vulnerable and fragile because we know that ink can fade and paper can be torn up or burnt . But the fact remains that they can last for a very long time . So they 're not as flimsy as you might think . <p> Digitalisation of records presumes a level of permanence . It certainly enables a level of access that we are n't accustomed to with paper . You do n't physically have to go to where the record is stored to find it . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ through reams of results . Who has n't searched on their own name just to find out what 's there ? <p> But there are two interesting points about this . The first is that if you try and search for someone who lived and died before this digitalisation , it can be very difficult to find anything . Yes , you 'll find some people but others have left not a trace online . Digitally , it 's as if they never existed . This despite the fact we who remember them know they existed , that they lived and had purpose . Unless you purposely search for them in the database of records for births and deaths , you might find nothing at all . <p> The only reason they might exist in the digital universe is because we remember them , we upload pictures , we write something about them . The Internet holds those memories but we supply them . <p> Does that mean they never existed ? Of course not . <p> By the same token , as we move further and further away from @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ where our existence could be erased with a click because there is nothing tangible holding them . We assume a permanence from the digital world that is n't necessarily there . In its own way , it can be just as fragile . Records can be deleted , they can be corrupted , they can be damaged . They are vulnerable . <p> Which opens up another interesting issue , what happens if we 're erased from the digital world but the physical world remembers us ? Or vice versa ? What happens if someone is born but dies within days in an airstrike , for example , along with their family , how do we know they existed ? What if all testament to their existence is obliterated but it persists , that they have identity because someone , possibly dead too , typed their name into a computer a few days earlier .

@@116920470 <h> Government scheme ' win-win ' for 118 schools - Scroll down to see if yours is included ! <p> Minister for Education , Norma Foley <p> Reporter : <p> Faye Dorgan <p> 31 Oct 2024 5:00 PM <p> OVER 100 schools in Limerick are set to be fitted with new solar PV panels . <p> 118 schools in Limerick city and county have qualified for the installation of new 6kW roof-mounted solar photovoltaic ( PV ) panels , which equates to approximately 14 units . <p> Since the launch of the first phase of the government programme late last year , 11 schools in Limerick were eligible for the scheme , which has jumped to 118 this time around in phase two , making the total number 129 . <p> The typical amount a school can save on electricity and utility bills will depend on a number of factors . <p> However , by way of example , a school with a 6kW Solar PV installation could expect to save in the region of ? 1200 to ? 1600 per annum on their bill , including credit for their electricity export . <p> The overall programme will see @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ schools with associated savings in the region of ? 5.5m per year across 4,000 schools . <p> Minister Collins said : " I am delighted to receive confirmation that 129 schools in Limerick have had free solar PV panels installed on their roofs under the Solar for Schools scheme . <p> " It is a win-win because it is saving schools money and reducing their carbon footprint . " <p> It comes as Minister for Education , Norma Foley , announced that the Solar for Schools Programme will be extended to all schools nationwide . <p> Minister Foley said : " The Solar for Schools Programme has delivered a rooftop revolution in more than 1,000 schools so far . <p> " It has helped these schools to reduce their carbon emissions . It also has the potential to save our schools millions of euro on their energy bills not just for one year , but every year . " <p> The Solar for Schools Programme is funded by the government 's Climate Action Fund and is in line with the government 's recognition that taking action on climate change is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ The solar PV panels in Limerick schools are being connected to the electricity grid , so that solar energy can power other homes and businesses when schools are closed at weekends and during school holidays . <p> ADVERTISEMENT - CONTINUE READING BELOW <p> ADVERTISEMENT - CONTINUE READING BELOW <p> ADVERTISEMENT - CONTINUE READING BELOW <p> ADVERTISEMENT - CONTINUE READING BELOW <p> ADVERTISEMENT - CONTINUE READING BELOW <p> ADVERTISEMENT - CONTINUE READING BELOW <p> ADVERTISEMENT - CONTINUE READING BELOW <p> ADVERTISEMENT - CONTINUE READING BELOW <p> ADVERTISEMENT - CONTINUE READING BELOW <p> ADVERTISEMENT - CONTINUE READING BELOW <p> ADVERTISEMENT - CONTINUE READING BELOW <p> 4 <p> To continue reading this article , please subscribe and support local journalism ! <p> Subscribing will allow you access to all of our premium content and archived articles . <p> Registration is absolutely 100% FREE and will help us personalise your experience on our sites . @@116923727 <h> Leonardo Dicaprio Donates $5 Million Dollars To The Amazon Fires <p> Leonardo DiCaprio has been a longtime environmental activist , he famously addressed the UN at the Paris Agreement signing ceremony in 2016 . <p> Now the Oscar-winning actor has donated $5 million dollars towards efforts to preserve the Amazon rainforest and combat wildfires . Dicaprio made the considerable pledge via the Earth Alliance , which is his own climate change-focused organization that was initiated in July . <p> Earth Alliance 's has just launched the Amazon Forest Fund and the actor claims the fund 's objective is to : <p> " help local partners and the indigenous communities on the front lines protecting the Amazon . " <p> CoS reports that the fund will offer assistance to the Instituto Associacao Floresta Protegida ( Kayapo ) and Coordination of the Indigenous Organizations of the Brazilian Amazon ( COIAB ) . <p> It 's reported that the fires are a result of mass deforestation by cattle farmers . It 's estimated there have been 74,000 recorded fires this year , an 84% surge from 2018 . <p> DiCaprio wrote on Instagram via Rainforest Alliance : <p> " Scientists and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ who issued an open invitation to loggers and farmers to clear the land after taking office in January . " <p> This is not the first time DiCaprio has spearheaded initiatives . Back in 1998 , he kick-started the Leonardo DiCaprio Foundation , a nonprofit organisation , its mission to spread environmental awareness . <p> And in 2016 , he was heavily involved in Before the Flood , the climate change documentary scored by NIN 's Trent Reznor and Atticus Ross . <h> NOVA.ie &amp; Radio NOVA <p> NOVA.ie and Radio NOVA is a premium publisher and broadcaster , based in Dublin , Ireland .
